    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23.2, F32.3, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I’m here because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Not like a vague memory or a thought in my own head — I mean as clearly as if he’s right there, standing behind me. It happens when I’m out on the trail, and it’s happened in my painting room too, where everything is quiet enough that I can hear every little creak, and then suddenly there’s him.\n\nAnd I know, at least part of me knows, that Garrett is dead. I do know that. But when it’s happening, it doesn’t feel like remembering him. It feels completely real, like he’s actually there and if I turn around fast enough I’ll catch him standing there. It’s... it’s been scaring me, honestly, because I can’t seem to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen Garrett or noticed any other unusual sights, smells, or sensations, or is it only his voice that you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly his voice. I mean, that’s the thing that keeps happening and the thing that feels… impossible to dismiss. I haven’t had some clear moment where I actually see Garrett standing there, like a full figure in front of me, at least not in the same way I hear him. It’s more that I hear him so clearly that my body reacts like he’s there — like I’ll turn or freeze because it feels like he’s right behind me.\n\nOn the trail, it’s especially bad because there’s space and silence, and then suddenly I’ll hear him, like his voice is coming from just over my shoulder. And in my painting room too — it’s quiet in there, almost too quiet sometimes — and then it’s like he’s in the room with me. I know he’s dead. I do know that when I’m talking about it now. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like grief or memory. It feels real. Completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen anything unusual or experienced other strange sensations, like seeing something that wasn’t there or smelling something odd, or has it been only Garrett’s voice that comes back this way?\n\nPatient: It’s really only Garrett’s voice that comes back like that. I don’t think I’ve seen him — not in the way people mean, like a figure standing there or something I can point to. And I haven’t noticed smells or anything like that.\n\nBut the voice is so clear that it almost makes the rest of it happen in my body, if that makes sense. Like when I’m on the trail, I’ll hear him behind me, close enough that I swear if I turn around he should be right there. Or in my painting room, when it’s quiet and I’m alone, it’ll just cut through everything — his voice, unmistakably his. And part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that sitting here talking to you, but in the moment it doesn’t land that way. It doesn’t feel like I’m remembering him. It feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something was true that others didn’t seem to agree with, or where things felt very strange or unreal in a way that was hard to explain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I know you’re asking about beliefs, like whether I’ve been convinced of something other people thought wasn’t true, and I don’t know that I’d put it that way.\n\nIt’s more that certain moments feel strange because of how real they feel to me, and then later I can sit here and explain them differently. But I don’t know if that counts as believing something exactly. I haven’t really been walking around with some fixed idea that everyone argues with me about, at least not that I can think of right now.\n\nThe “unreal” part… maybe. Sometimes the whole situation feels hard to explain because I’ll be completely aware afterward that something doesn’t make sense, but while it’s happening my body reacts before I can reason through it. So I guess I’d say I’ve had experiences that feel strange and hard to explain, but I don’t know if I’ve had a belief like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something that turned out not to be true, but at the time you were completely sure it was?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I don’t want to say no too quickly just because I’m embarrassed or because I’m missing something obvious. But… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a specific belief where later everyone was like, “That was clearly not true,” and I had been absolutely convinced.\n\nThere are moments where I feel completely sure of what I’m experiencing while it’s happening, and then afterward I can question it more. But that’s not quite the same as having a whole belief, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not aware of having some fixed idea that I built my life around and then found out was false.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. Nothing specific is coming to mind. If you mean something more subtle, you may have to help me understand what kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you were absolutely certain something was happening or true, even though there was no evidence for it and others didn’t share that belief?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in that clear-cut way, at least. I’m trying to be careful because “absolutely certain” is a strong phrase, and I don’t want to dodge it just because it makes me uncomfortable.\n\nThere are times when something feels completely real to me in the moment, and I react as if it’s real before I can step back and evaluate it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve had a belief like, “This is definitely happening,” with no evidence, and other people were telling me it wasn’t true, and I stayed convinced anyway… I can’t really think of an example.\n\nPart of what’s confusing is that when I’m inside the experience, it doesn’t feel like I’m forming a belief. It’s more immediate than that. Then afterward, I can look at it and say, “Okay, maybe that wasn’t what it felt like.” But I don’t know that I’ve had something where I was arguing with people or holding onto an idea despite everyone else saying no.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but nothing like that is coming to mind. If there’s a specific kind of thing you mean, I might be able to answer better.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of time where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, there have been stretches like that. It’s not just “sad,” exactly, or not only sad. It’s more like everything that normally gives me some sense of being myself just goes dull.\n\nThe things that usually anchor me — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even my work, even sitting with patients — they start to feel flat and unreachable. I can still physically do some of those things, sometimes, but it feels like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. Like I know, intellectually, that these are the things I care about, or used to care about, but I can’t feel the connection to them.\n\nAnd it’s most of the day, not just a bad evening. It kind of colors the whole day. I’ll look at a canvas or pick up the guitar and there’s this blankness, like the emotional part of me that’s supposed to respond just isn’t available. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s frightening because those things are normally how I find my way back to myself. When even those feel pointless, it starts to feel pretty hopeless.\n\nDoctor: During these periods when you felt down and lost interest in things, did you also experience a loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, or a lack of reactivity to usually pleasurable stimuli?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It isn’t just that I’m “not in the mood” for things — it’s like the pleasure system goes offline, if that makes sense.\n\nThe things that normally anchor me just… don’t reach me. Painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even the part of my work where I’m sitting with patients and usually feel connected to something meaningful — all of that starts to feel flat and far away. I can still sometimes do the actions, like I can pick up the guitar or stand in front of a canvas, but there’s no spark. It’s like I’m performing the shape of my life without feeling any of the reason for it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me, honestly, because those are not casual hobbies to me. They’re usually how I know I’m still here, how I orient myself. So when even they feel unreachable, it’s not just boredom. It feels like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you believed something was happening or real, even though it wasn’t based on anything actually occurring in the outside world?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the main reason I’m here, honestly.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Not like a memory exactly, and not like when you “hear” someone in your head because you know them so well. It’s much more immediate than that. It sounds as clear as if he’s actually standing behind me.\n\nIt happens on the trail sometimes, or when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be alone, and then I hear him — his voice, specifically — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this jolt like someone has just come into the room. And the strange part is that part of me knows he’s dead. I know that. I can say it right now and understand it. But when it’s happening, it feels completely real. Not symbolic, not imagined on purpose, not just grief in an abstract way. Real, like he’s there.\n\nAfterward I can try to explain it to myself and say, “Okay, there wasn’t anyone there, nothing actually happened in the room,” but during it, it doesn’t feel like I’m making something up. It feels like the outside world has changed for a second, like he’s right there behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like seeing objects or people that others don't see, feeling sensations on your body that have no explanation, or believing something is true that turns out to be completely disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the main thing is hearing Garrett.\n\nI don’t really have a better way to describe it than that — I hear his voice. And it’s not the same as remembering what he sounded like, or having a thought in my own mind that uses his voice. It’s… outside of me, or it feels that way. Like if I’m on the trail, or in my painting room, and suddenly he’s just behind me. Clear. Immediate. As if he’s standing there and said something.\n\nAnd I know — sitting here, talking to you — I know he’s dead. I understand that. But when it happens, that knowledge doesn’t seem to matter fast enough. My body reacts like he’s really there. I’ll turn around or freeze, and for those moments it feels completely real, not like imagination or a metaphor or grief in some poetic sense.\n\nThen afterward I can tell myself, “No, there was no one there, nothing actually happened,” but in the moment it feels like the world has changed around me. That’s why it scares me. Because part of me knows it can’t be real, but another part experiences it as real anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief about something happening or being true that you later realized had no basis in reality at all, and wasn't related to hearing Garrett's voice or thinking about his death?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with how I answer, because I don’t want to give you a polished answer just because I know what the question is getting at. But if you’re asking whether I’ve had some separate fixed belief — like being convinced something was happening that later turned out to have no basis — outside of Garrett’s voice or thoughts around his death, I don’t think so.\n\nI’ve had strong feelings, and I’ve misread things emotionally, I’m sure. I can spiral or get very certain internally when I’m distressed. But in terms of believing something was literally true in the outside world and then realizing it wasn’t real at all… I can’t think of an example that isn’t connected to hearing him or feeling like he’s there.\n\nSo, no — not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you believed something was happening or true that wasn’t based on anything real, and wasn’t related to hearing Garrett’s voice or thinking he was there?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I know I’ve sort of already answered that, but I’m trying to really check it against my memory and not just say no automatically.\n\nThe Garrett thing is the only experience that feels like that to me — where something seems real in the room or on the trail and then afterward I can say, “No, that wasn’t actually happening.” Outside of that, I don’t have another example where I believed some whole separate thing was true and then later realized it had no basis in reality.\n\nI mean, I can get very caught up in my own interpretations of things, especially when I’m upset, but that feels different. That feels like emotion or anxiety or reading too much into something, not like I’m convinced of a concrete thing happening in the outside world that isn’t real.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no — not apart from hearing him or feeling like he’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs during these experiences that you were being punished, deserved to suffer, or were responsible for something terrible, even if you couldn’t explain exactly what?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part is… unfortunately very familiar.\n\nIt’s not always a clean thought like, “I am being punished,” exactly, though sometimes it gets close to that. It’s more like this heavy, grinding certainty that I failed in some fundamental way. I keep going back over every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something in his voice, whether I should have called again, stayed longer, asked a different question, been less self-absorbed. It’s like my mind won’t let the tape stop. It just keeps rewinding to the same moments and saying, “There. That’s where you failed him. There. That’s where you should have known.”\n\nAnd it doesn’t stay only with Garrett. It spreads. I start thinking about every patient session I cancelled, every person who needed me and didn’t get me at my best, or didn’t get me at all because I couldn’t show up. And then it becomes this… global verdict, I guess. Like I didn’t just make mistakes — like I’m the mistake. Like everyone around me has to work around the damage I cause.\n\nWhen I’m in that state, I can convince myself pretty thoroughly that people would be better off if I weren’t in the way. Not in some abstract dramatic sense — it feels very practical in the moment, like I’m a burden, like I contaminate things by being part of them. And I know that sounds extreme saying it out loud. I can hear how it sounds. But when I’m in it, it feels deserved. Like suffering is the appropriate consequence for failing everyone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes — Garrett’s voice. That’s the main thing. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it isn’t just like remembering what he sounded like, or having an intrusive thought in his voice. It’s… it sounds external. It sounds like he’s actually there.

It happens most clearly when I’m on the trail, especially if I’m alone, or in my painting room. I’ll hear him behind me or off to the side, clear enough that my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. I’ll turn around, or I’ll stop what I’m doing, because in that split second it feels exactly like someone standing there talking to me.

And then there’s this awful second after, where part of me knows he’s dead. I know that. I know it sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like memory. It doesn’t feel symbolic. It feels completely real, like he’s right behind me and I’m just about to see him if I turn fast enough.

As far as other voices or sounds, I don’t think there’s anything separate like that. And I don’t think I’ve seen things in the same way. It’s really his voice — and the sense that he’s there with it — that’s been happening.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I’m here because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Not like a vague memory or a thought in my own head — I mean as clearly as if he’s right there, standing behind me. It happens when I’m out on the trail, and it’s happened in my painting room too, where everything is quiet enough that I can hear every little creak, and then suddenly there’s him.\n\nAnd I know, at least part of me knows, that Garrett is dead. I do know that. But when it’s happening, it doesn’t feel like remembering him. It feels completely real, like he’s actually there and if I turn around fast enough I’ll catch him standing there. It’s... it’s been scaring me, honestly, because I can’t seem to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen Garrett or noticed any other unusual sights, smells, or sensations, or is it only his voice that you hear?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly his voice. I mean, that’s the thing that keeps happening and the thing that feels… impossible to dismiss. I haven’t had some clear moment where I actually see Garrett standing there, like a full figure in front of me, at least not in the same way I hear him. It’s more that I hear him so clearly that my body reacts like he’s there — like I’ll turn or freeze because it feels like he’s right behind me.\n\nOn the trail, it’s especially bad because there’s space and silence, and then suddenly I’ll hear him, like his voice is coming from just over my shoulder. And in my painting room too — it’s quiet in there, almost too quiet sometimes — and then it’s like he’s in the room with me. I know he’s dead. I do know that when I’m talking about it now. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like grief or memory. It feels real. Completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen anything unusual or experienced other strange sensations, like seeing something that wasn’t there or smelling something odd, or has it been only Garrett’s voice that comes back this way?\n\nPatient: It’s really only Garrett’s voice that comes back like that. I don’t think I’ve seen him — not in the way people mean, like a figure standing there or something I can point to. And I haven’t noticed smells or anything like that.\n\nBut the voice is so clear that it almost makes the rest of it happen in my body, if that makes sense. Like when I’m on the trail, I’ll hear him behind me, close enough that I swear if I turn around he should be right there. Or in my painting room, when it’s quiet and I’m alone, it’ll just cut through everything — his voice, unmistakably his. And part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that sitting here talking to you, but in the moment it doesn’t land that way. It doesn’t feel like I’m remembering him. It feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something was true that others didn’t seem to agree with, or where things felt very strange or unreal in a way that was hard to explain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I know you’re asking about beliefs, like whether I’ve been convinced of something other people thought wasn’t true, and I don’t know that I’d put it that way.\n\nIt’s more that certain moments feel strange because of how real they feel to me, and then later I can sit here and explain them differently. But I don’t know if that counts as believing something exactly. I haven’t really been walking around with some fixed idea that everyone argues with me about, at least not that I can think of right now.\n\nThe “unreal” part… maybe. Sometimes the whole situation feels hard to explain because I’ll be completely aware afterward that something doesn’t make sense, but while it’s happening my body reacts before I can reason through it. So I guess I’d say I’ve had experiences that feel strange and hard to explain, but I don’t know if I’ve had a belief like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something that turned out not to be true, but at the time you were completely sure it was?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I don’t want to say no too quickly just because I’m embarrassed or because I’m missing something obvious. But… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a specific belief where later everyone was like, “That was clearly not true,” and I had been absolutely convinced.\n\nThere are moments where I feel completely sure of what I’m experiencing while it’s happening, and then afterward I can question it more. But that’s not quite the same as having a whole belief, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not aware of having some fixed idea that I built my life around and then found out was false.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. Nothing specific is coming to mind. If you mean something more subtle, you may have to help me understand what kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you were absolutely certain something was happening or true, even though there was no evidence for it and others didn’t share that belief?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in that clear-cut way, at least. I’m trying to be careful because “absolutely certain” is a strong phrase, and I don’t want to dodge it just because it makes me uncomfortable.\n\nThere are times when something feels completely real to me in the moment, and I react as if it’s real before I can step back and evaluate it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve had a belief like, “This is definitely happening,” with no evidence, and other people were telling me it wasn’t true, and I stayed convinced anyway… I can’t really think of an example.\n\nPart of what’s confusing is that when I’m inside the experience, it doesn’t feel like I’m forming a belief. It’s more immediate than that. Then afterward, I can look at it and say, “Okay, maybe that wasn’t what it felt like.” But I don’t know that I’ve had something where I was arguing with people or holding onto an idea despite everyone else saying no.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but nothing like that is coming to mind. If there’s a specific kind of thing you mean, I might be able to answer better.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of time where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, there have been stretches like that. It’s not just “sad,” exactly, or not only sad. It’s more like everything that normally gives me some sense of being myself just goes dull.\n\nThe things that usually anchor me — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even my work, even sitting with patients — they start to feel flat and unreachable. I can still physically do some of those things, sometimes, but it feels like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. Like I know, intellectually, that these are the things I care about, or used to care about, but I can’t feel the connection to them.\n\nAnd it’s most of the day, not just a bad evening. It kind of colors the whole day. I’ll look at a canvas or pick up the guitar and there’s this blankness, like the emotional part of me that’s supposed to respond just isn’t available. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s frightening because those things are normally how I find my way back to myself. When even those feel pointless, it starts to feel pretty hopeless.\n\nDoctor: During these periods when you felt down and lost interest in things, did you also experience a loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, or a lack of reactivity to usually pleasurable stimuli?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It isn’t just that I’m “not in the mood” for things — it’s like the pleasure system goes offline, if that makes sense.\n\nThe things that normally anchor me just… don’t reach me. Painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even the part of my work where I’m sitting with patients and usually feel connected to something meaningful — all of that starts to feel flat and far away. I can still sometimes do the actions, like I can pick up the guitar or stand in front of a canvas, but there’s no spark. It’s like I’m performing the shape of my life without feeling any of the reason for it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me, honestly, because those are not casual hobbies to me. They’re usually how I know I’m still here, how I orient myself. So when even they feel unreachable, it’s not just boredom. It feels like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you believed something was happening or real, even though it wasn’t based on anything actually occurring in the outside world?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the main reason I’m here, honestly.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Not like a memory exactly, and not like when you “hear” someone in your head because you know them so well. It’s much more immediate than that. It sounds as clear as if he’s actually standing behind me.\n\nIt happens on the trail sometimes, or when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be alone, and then I hear him — his voice, specifically — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this jolt like someone has just come into the room. And the strange part is that part of me knows he’s dead. I know that. I can say it right now and understand it. But when it’s happening, it feels completely real. Not symbolic, not imagined on purpose, not just grief in an abstract way. Real, like he’s there.\n\nAfterward I can try to explain it to myself and say, “Okay, there wasn’t anyone there, nothing actually happened in the room,” but during it, it doesn’t feel like I’m making something up. It feels like the outside world has changed for a second, like he’s right there behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like seeing objects or people that others don't see, feeling sensations on your body that have no explanation, or believing something is true that turns out to be completely disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the main thing is hearing Garrett.\n\nI don’t really have a better way to describe it than that — I hear his voice. And it’s not the same as remembering what he sounded like, or having a thought in my own mind that uses his voice. It’s… outside of me, or it feels that way. Like if I’m on the trail, or in my painting room, and suddenly he’s just behind me. Clear. Immediate. As if he’s standing there and said something.\n\nAnd I know — sitting here, talking to you — I know he’s dead. I understand that. But when it happens, that knowledge doesn’t seem to matter fast enough. My body reacts like he’s really there. I’ll turn around or freeze, and for those moments it feels completely real, not like imagination or a metaphor or grief in some poetic sense.\n\nThen afterward I can tell myself, “No, there was no one there, nothing actually happened,” but in the moment it feels like the world has changed around me. That’s why it scares me. Because part of me knows it can’t be real, but another part experiences it as real anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief about something happening or being true that you later realized had no basis in reality at all, and wasn't related to hearing Garrett's voice or thinking about his death?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with how I answer, because I don’t want to give you a polished answer just because I know what the question is getting at. But if you’re asking whether I’ve had some separate fixed belief — like being convinced something was happening that later turned out to have no basis — outside of Garrett’s voice or thoughts around his death, I don’t think so.\n\nI’ve had strong feelings, and I’ve misread things emotionally, I’m sure. I can spiral or get very certain internally when I’m distressed. But in terms of believing something was literally true in the outside world and then realizing it wasn’t real at all… I can’t think of an example that isn’t connected to hearing him or feeling like he’s there.\n\nSo, no — not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you believed something was happening or true that wasn’t based on anything real, and wasn’t related to hearing Garrett’s voice or thinking he was there?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I know I’ve sort of already answered that, but I’m trying to really check it against my memory and not just say no automatically.\n\nThe Garrett thing is the only experience that feels like that to me — where something seems real in the room or on the trail and then afterward I can say, “No, that wasn’t actually happening.” Outside of that, I don’t have another example where I believed some whole separate thing was true and then later realized it had no basis in reality.\n\nI mean, I can get very caught up in my own interpretations of things, especially when I’m upset, but that feels different. That feels like emotion or anxiety or reading too much into something, not like I’m convinced of a concrete thing happening in the outside world that isn’t real.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no — not apart from hearing him or feeling like he’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs during these experiences that you were being punished, deserved to suffer, or were responsible for something terrible, even if you couldn’t explain exactly what?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part is… unfortunately very familiar.\n\nIt’s not always a clean thought like, “I am being punished,” exactly, though sometimes it gets close to that. It’s more like this heavy, grinding certainty that I failed in some fundamental way. I keep going back over every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something in his voice, whether I should have called again, stayed longer, asked a different question, been less self-absorbed. It’s like my mind won’t let the tape stop. It just keeps rewinding to the same moments and saying, “There. That’s where you failed him. There. That’s where you should have known.”\n\nAnd it doesn’t stay only with Garrett. It spreads. I start thinking about every patient session I cancelled, every person who needed me and didn’t get me at my best, or didn’t get me at all because I couldn’t show up. And then it becomes this… global verdict, I guess. Like I didn’t just make mistakes — like I’m the mistake. Like everyone around me has to work around the damage I cause.\n\nWhen I’m in that state, I can convince myself pretty thoroughly that people would be better off if I weren’t in the way. Not in some abstract dramatic sense — it feels very practical in the moment, like I’m a burden, like I contaminate things by being part of them. And I know that sounds extreme saying it out loud. I can hear how it sounds. But when I’m in it, it feels deserved. Like suffering is the appropriate consequence for failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other voices or sounds that others couldn’t hear, or seen things that others said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: Yes — Garrett’s voice. That’s the main thing. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it isn’t just like remembering what he sounded like, or having an intrusive thought in his voice. It’s… it sounds external. It sounds like he’s actually there.\n\nIt happens most clearly when I’m on the trail, especially if I’m alone, or in my painting room. I’ll hear him behind me or off to the side, clear enough that my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. I’ll turn around, or I’ll stop what I’m doing, because in that split second it feels exactly like someone standing there talking to me.\n\nAnd then there’s this awful second after, where part of me knows he’s dead. I know that. I know it sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like memory. It doesn’t feel symbolic. It feels completely real, like he’s right behind me and I’m just about to see him if I turn fast enough.\n\nAs far as other voices or sounds, I don’t think there’s anything separate like that. And I don’t think I’ve seen things in the same way. It’s really his voice — and the sense that he’s there with it — that’s been happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s really still Garrett’s voice. I know I’ve probably said that a few different ways now, but that’s the thing that feels unusual in the way you mean.

I’ll be on the trail, or in my painting room, and I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me or just off to the side. Not like a memory. Not like I’m thinking, “Oh, I can imagine what Garrett would say.” It has that outside-of-me quality, where my body believes it before my mind has time to argue with it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or listen for another word, because for that instant it feels completely real.

And then almost immediately there’s this horrible split where part of me knows he’s dead — I do know that — but the experience itself doesn’t feel like knowing he’s dead. It feels like he’s there and I’m about to see him if I turn around fast enough. That’s the part that scares me, I think. The certainty of it in the moment.

I don’t have another voice I can point to, or some separate kind of perception that’s as clear as that. It’s Garrett’s voice, and that sense of him being there with it.

That part is… unfortunately very familiar.\n\nIt’s not always a clean thought like, “I am being punished,” exactly, though sometimes it gets close to that. It’s more like this heavy, grinding certainty that I failed in some fundamental way. I keep going back over every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something in his voice, whether I should have called again, stayed longer, asked a different question, been less self-absorbed. It’s like my mind won’t let the tape stop. It just keeps rewinding to the same moments and saying, “There. That’s where you failed him. There. That’s where you should have known.”\n\nAnd it doesn’t stay only with Garrett. It spreads. I start thinking about every patient session I cancelled, every person who needed me and didn’t get me at my best, or didn’t get me at all because I couldn’t show up. And then it becomes this… global verdict, I guess. Like I didn’t just make mistakes — like I’m the mistake. Like everyone around me has to work around the damage I cause.\n\nWhen I’m in that state, I can convince myself pretty thoroughly that people would be better off if I weren’t in the way. Not in some abstract dramatic sense — it feels very practical in the moment, like I’m a burden, like I contaminate things by being part of them. And I know that sounds extreme saying it out loud. I can hear how it sounds. But when I’m in it, it feels deserved. Like suffering is the appropriate consequence for failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other voices or sounds that others couldn’t hear, or seen things that others said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: Yes — Garrett’s voice. That’s the main thing. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it isn’t just like remembering what he sounded like, or having an intrusive thought in his voice. It’s… it sounds external. It sounds like he’s actually there.\n\nIt happens most clearly when I’m on the trail, especially if I’m alone, or in my painting room. I’ll hear him behind me or off to the side, clear enough that my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. I’ll turn around, or I’ll stop what I’m doing, because in that split second it feels exactly like someone standing there talking to me.\n\nAnd then there’s this awful second after, where part of me knows he’s dead. I know that. I know it sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like memory. It doesn’t feel symbolic. It feels completely real, like he’s right behind me and I’m just about to see him if I turn fast enough.\n\nAs far as other voices or sounds, I don’t think there’s anything separate like that. And I don’t think I’ve seen things in the same way. It’s really his voice — and the sense that he’s there with it — that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other unusual perceptions — like seeing things, feeling things on your body, or hearing other voices — that others couldn’t notice or didn’t agree with?\n\nPatient: It’s really still Garrett’s voice. I know I’ve probably said that a few different ways now, but that’s the thing that feels unusual in the way you mean.\n\nI’ll be on the trail, or in my painting room, and I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me or just off to the side. Not like a memory. Not like I’m thinking, “Oh, I can imagine what Garrett would say.” It has that outside-of-me quality, where my body believes it before my mind has time to argue with it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or listen for another word, because for that instant it feels completely real.\n\nAnd then almost immediately there’s this horrible split where part of me knows he’s dead — I do know that — but the experience itself doesn’t feel like knowing he’s dead. It feels like he’s there and I’m about to see him if I turn around fast enough. That’s the part that scares me, I think. The certainty of it in the moment.\n\nI don’t have another voice I can point to, or some separate kind of perception that’s as clear as that. It’s Garrett’s voice, and that sense of him being there with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

And then it becomes this… global verdict, I guess. Like I didn’t just make mistakes — like I’m the mistake. Like everyone around me has to work around the damage I cause.\n\nWhen I’m in that state, I can convince myself pretty thoroughly that people would be better off if I weren’t in the way. Not in some abstract dramatic sense — it feels very practical in the moment, like I’m a burden, like I contaminate things by being part of them. And I know that sounds extreme saying it out loud. I can hear how it sounds. But when I’m in it, it feels deserved. Like suffering is the appropriate consequence for failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other voices or sounds that others couldn’t hear, or seen things that others said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: Yes — Garrett’s voice. It sounds like he’s actually there.\n\nIt happens most clearly when I’m on the trail, especially if I’m alone, or in my painting room. I’ll hear him behind me or off to the side, clear enough that my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. It’s really his voice — and the sense that he’s there with it — that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other unusual perceptions — like seeing things, feeling things on your body, or hearing other voices — that others couldn’t notice or didn’t agree with?\n\nPatient: It’s really still Garrett’s voice. I know I’ve probably said that a few different ways now, but that’s the thing that feels unusual in the way you mean.\n\nI’ll be on the trail, or in my painting room, and I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me or just off to the side. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Not in the same way as hearing Garrett, but yes — the guilt gets so absolute that at the time it feels like a fact, not a feeling.

I’ll get locked into this loop where I replay every conversation with Garrett, every little pause, every tone of voice, every thing I said that might have been selfish or careless or too distracted. And then it branches out from there into work — every patient session I cancelled, every time I wasn’t fully present, every person who maybe needed me and I wasn’t there in the way I should have been. It becomes this whole case I’m building against myself, except in the moment I don’t experience it as “I’m thinking harshly about myself.” I experience it as, “This is the truth. I failed everyone.”

And when I’m inside that, I can become completely convinced that people would be better off if I just weren’t in the way. Like my presence itself is the problem. Later, when I’m more grounded, I can see that it’s not a fair or accurate conclusion — that I’m connecting things in this extreme way and treating every mistake or absence as proof that I’m fundamentally harmful. But at the time it feels airtight. Like there’s no other possible interpretation.

I’ll turn, or freeze, or listen for another word, because for that instant it feels completely real.\n\nAnd then almost immediately there’s this horrible split where part of me knows he’s dead — I do know that — but the experience itself doesn’t feel like knowing he’s dead. It feels like he’s there and I’m about to see him if I turn around fast enough. That’s the part that scares me, I think. The certainty of it in the moment.\n\nI don’t have another voice I can point to, or some separate kind of perception that’s as clear as that. It’s Garrett’s voice, and that sense of him being there with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was completely certain to you at the time, but later realized had no basis in reality at all, and wasn’t about Garrett being present or speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not in the same way as hearing Garrett, but yes — the guilt gets so absolute that at the time it feels like a fact, not a feeling.\n\nI’ll get locked into this loop where I replay every conversation with Garrett, every little pause, every tone of voice, every thing I said that might have been selfish or careless or too distracted. And then it branches out from there into work — every patient session I cancelled, every time I wasn’t fully present, every person who maybe needed me and I wasn’t there in the way I should have been. It becomes this whole case I’m building against myself, except in the moment I don’t experience it as “I’m thinking harshly about myself.” I experience it as, “This is the truth. I failed everyone.”\n\nAnd when I’m inside that, I can become completely convinced that people would be better off if I just weren’t in the way. Like my presence itself is the problem. Later, when I’m more grounded, I can see that it’s not a fair or accurate conclusion — that I’m connecting things in this extreme way and treating every mistake or absence as proof that I’m fundamentally harmful. But at the time it feels airtight. It’s really his voice — and the sense that he’s there with it — that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other unusual perceptions — like seeing things, feeling things on your body, or hearing other voices — that others couldn’t notice or didn’t agree with?\n\nPatient: It’s really still Garrett’s voice. Not like a memory. Not like I’m thinking, “Oh, I can imagine what Garrett would say.” It has that outside-of-me quality, where my body believes it before my mind has time to argue with it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or listen for another word, because for that instant it feels completely real.\n\nAnd then almost immediately there’s this horrible split where part of me knows he’s dead — I do know that — but the experience itself doesn’t feel like knowing he’s dead. It feels like he’s there and I’m about to see him if I turn around fast enough. That’s the part that scares me, I think. The certainty of it in the moment.\n\nI don’t have another voice I can point to, or some separate kind of perception that’s as clear as that. It’s Garrett’s voice, and that sense of him being there with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was completely certain to you at the time, but later realized had no basis in reality at all, and wasn’t about Garrett being present or speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not in the same way as hearing Garrett, but yes — the guilt gets so absolute that at the time it feels like a fact, not a feeling.\n\nI’ll get locked into this loop where I replay every conversation with Garrett, every little pause, every tone of voice, every thing I said that might have been selfish or careless or too distracted. And then it branches out from there into work — every patient session I cancelled, every time I wasn’t fully present, every person who maybe needed me and I wasn’t there in the way I should have been. It becomes this whole case I’m building against myself, except in the moment I don’t experience it as “I’m thinking harshly about myself.” I experience it as, “This is the truth. I failed everyone.”\n\nAnd when I’m inside that, I can become completely convinced that people would be better off if I just weren’t in the way. Like my presence itself is the problem. Later, when I’m more grounded, I can see that it’s not a fair or accurate conclusion — that I’m connecting things in this extreme way and treating every mistake or absence as proof that I’m fundamentally harmful. But at the time it feels airtight. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing.

I mean, I do get very self-conscious when I’m doing badly, and I can read too much into someone’s tone or a pause in a conversation — especially because I know I’m not exactly easy to be around when I’m like this. But I don’t think I’ve had the experience of believing strangers were watching me, or that the TV or signs or random events were sending me messages, or anything like that.

If anything, it’s more inward. I assume the worst about myself, or I feel exposed, like people can probably tell I’m falling apart. But that’s not the same as feeling like there’s some outside pattern aimed at me. I haven’t noticed that, no.

It becomes this whole case I’m building against myself, except in the moment I don’t experience it as “I’m thinking harshly about myself.” I experience it as, “This is the truth. I failed everyone.”\n\nAnd when I’m inside that, I can become completely convinced that people would be better off if I just weren’t in the way. Like my presence itself is the problem. Later, when I’m more grounded, I can see that it’s not a fair or accurate conclusion — that I’m connecting things in this extreme way and treating every mistake or absence as proof that I’m fundamentally harmful. But at the time it feels airtight. Like there’s no other possible interpretation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief during these difficult times that something strange or unusual was happening in the world around you — like people were watching you, talking about you, or that events had special meanings directed at you — even if you later realized it wasn’t actually true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. But I don’t think I’ve had the experience of believing strangers were watching me, or that the TV or signs or random events were sending me messages, or anything like that.\n\nIf anything, it’s more inward. I assume the worst about myself, or I feel exposed, like people can probably tell I’m falling apart. But that’s not the same as feeling like there’s some outside pattern aimed at me.   },

I’ll turn, or freeze, or listen for another word, because for that instant it feels completely real.\n\nAnd then almost immediately there’s this horrible split where part of me knows he’s dead — I do know that — but the experience itself doesn’t feel like knowing he’s dead. It feels like he’s there and I’m about to see him if I turn around fast enough. That’s the part that scares me, I think. The certainty of it in the moment.\n\nI don’t have another voice I can point to, or some separate kind of perception that’s as clear as that. It’s Garrett’s voice, and that sense of him being there with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was completely certain to you at the time, but later realized had no basis in reality at all, and wasn’t about Garrett being present or speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not in the same way as hearing Garrett, but yes — the guilt gets so absolute that at the time it feels like a fact, not a feeling.\n\nI’ll get locked into this loop where I replay every conversation with Garrett, every little pause, every tone of voice, every thing I said that might have been selfish or careless or too distracted. And then it branches out from there into work — every patient session I cancelled, every time I wasn’t fully present, every person who maybe needed me and I wasn’t there in the way I should have been. It becomes this whole case I’m building against myself, except in the moment I don’t experience it as “I’m thinking harshly about myself.” I experience it as, “This is the truth. I failed everyone.”\n\nAnd when I’m inside that, I can become completely convinced that people would be better off if I just weren’t in the way. Like my presence itself is the problem. Later, when I’m more grounded, I can see that it’s not a fair or accurate conclusion — that I’m connecting things in this extreme way and treating every mistake or absence as proof that I’m fundamentally harmful. But at the time it feels airtight. Like there’s no other possible interpretation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief during these difficult times that something strange or unusual was happening in the world around you — like people were watching you, talking about you, or that events had special meanings directed at you — even if you later realized it wasn’t actually true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, I do get very self-conscious when I’m doing badly, and I can read too much into someone’s tone or a pause in a conversation — especially because I know I’m not exactly easy to be around when I’m like this. But I don’t think I’ve had the experience of believing strangers were watching me, or that the TV or signs or random events were sending me messages, or anything like that.\n\nIf anything, it’s more inward. I assume the worst about myself, or I feel exposed, like people can probably tell I’m falling apart. But that’s not the same as feeling like there’s some outside pattern aimed at me. I haven’t noticed that, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a belief during these episodes that something about you or your life was fundamentally changed in a way that wasn't about guilt or deserving punishment — like your body was different, your mind wasn't yours, or the world had shifted in a way no one else seemed to notice?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, I don’t think so. I’m trying to think carefully because some of those words sound close to things I feel when I’m really distressed — like feeling unreal, or feeling like I can’t trust my own mind — but I don’t think I’ve had a fixed belief that my body had changed, or that my mind wasn’t mine, or that the world had literally shifted in some way.
